=== SOURCE CODE (java) ===
package org.example;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class CombinationCalculator {
    private List<String> datas;

    public CombinationCalculator(List<String> datas) {
        this.datas = datas;
    }

    public static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return factorial(n) / (factorial(n - m) * factorial(m));
    }

    private static int factorial(int x) {
        int result = 1;
        for (int i = 1; i <= x; i++) {
            result *= i;
        }
        return result;
    }

    public static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return Double.NaN;
        }
        return n != 63 ? (1 << n) - 1 : Double.POSITIVE_INFINITY;
    }

    public List<List<String>> select(int m) {
        List<List<String>> result = new ArrayList<>();
        _select(0, new ArrayList<>(m), 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    public List<List<String>> selectAll() {
        List<List<String>> result = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 1; i <= datas.size(); i++) {
            result.addAll(select(i));
        }
        return result;
    }

    protected void _select(int dataIndex, List<String> resultList, int resultIndex, List<List<String>> result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.add(new ArrayList<>(resultList));
            return;
        }

        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= datas.size() - (m - resultIndex); i++) {
            resultList.add(resultIndex, datas.get(i));
            _select(i + 1, resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.remove(resultIndex);
        }
    }
}


=== PROMPT ===
You are an expert programmer. Translate the following java code to cpp.
- Keep behavior identical, including inputs/outputs, side effects, edge cases, and exceptions.
- Use idiomatic cpp only when it does not change behavior.

```java
package org.example;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class CombinationCalculator {
    private List<String> datas;

    public CombinationCalculator(List<String> datas) {
        this.datas = datas;
    }

    public static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return factorial(n) / (factorial(n - m) * factorial(m));
    }

    private static int factorial(int x) {
        int result = 1;
        for (int i = 1; i <= x; i++) {
            result *= i;
        }
        return result;
    }

    public static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return Double.NaN;
        }
        return n != 63 ? (1 << n) - 1 : Double.POSITIVE_INFINITY;
    }

    public List<List<String>> select(int m) {
        List<List<String>> result = new ArrayList<>();
        _select(0, new ArrayList<>(m), 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    public List<List<String>> selectAll() {
        List<List<String>> result = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 1; i <= datas.size(); i++) {
            result.addAll(select(i));
        }
        return result;
    }

    protected void _select(int dataIndex, List<String> resultList, int resultIndex, List<List<String>> result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.add(new ArrayList<>(resultList));
            return;
        }

        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= datas.size() - (m - resultIndex); i++) {
            resultList.add(resultIndex, datas.get(i));
            _select(i + 1, resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.remove(resultIndex);
        }
    }
}

```

Use a short stable mapping path that preserves behavior.

Keep the reasoning compact and single-pass:
- Identify only the pieces that must remain equivalent: signatures, I/O contract, stateful behavior, control flow, and key API semantics.
- Turn that mapping into target-language structure promptly.
- Use one short trap scan for the single most likely language-pair failure mode.
- If a mismatch appears, patch only the affected local section and recheck once.
- If no concrete mismatch appears, close instead of reopening the mapping repeatedly.

Output only the translated cpp code in a code block.

Potential C++ code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cmath>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas; // or pointer?
public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) return 1;
        return factorial(n) / (factorial(n - m) * factorial(m));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        if (n == 63) return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        // Java: (1 << n) - 1 with int shift semantics
        int shift = n & 31;
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << shift) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m); // Java new ArrayList<>(m) sets capacity, not size
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        for (int i = 1; i <= (int)datas.size(); i++) {
            auto selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= (int)datas.size() - (m - resultIndex); i++) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(i + 1, resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }

private:
    static int factorial(int x) {
        int result = 1;
        for (int i = 1; i <= x; i++) {
            result = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(result) * static_cast<uint32_t>(i));
        }
        return result;
    }
};

Wait, in count, factorial(n) / (factorial(n-m) * factorial(m)). The multiplication of factorial(n-m) * factorial(m) could overflow. In Java, multiplication is int multiplication with overflow. In C++, we need to compute the product with wrap semantics. We can write a helper multiply(int a, int b) returning int32_t with wrap. Then denominator = multiply(factorial(n-m), factorial(m)). Then division: numerator / denominator. In C++, signed division of int32_t is defined for all values except INT_MIN / -1. If denominator is 0? factorial never 0 for non-negative x? factorial(0)=1, factorial(negative)=1. So denominator not zero. If denominator is -1 and numerator is INT_MIN, division overflows? Actually INT_MIN / -1 is undefined in C++ and throws? In Java, it results in INT_MIN (overflow). This could happen if factorial values produce such values. To be fully identical, we need to handle INT_MIN / -1. But is it possible? Let's see: factorial values modulo 2^32. For some n, factorial could be INT_MIN? Possibly. If denominator = -1, numerator = INT_MIN, Java returns INT_MIN. C++ signed division would be UB. To avoid, we can perform division using unsigned? But as noted, unsigned division of the bit patterns gives wrong result for negative values. We need a function that mimics Java's int division exactly, including overflow. Java int division rounds toward zero. For INT_MIN / -1, Java returns INT_MIN. In C++, we can check if denominator == -1 and numerator == INT_MIN, return INT_MIN. Otherwise, do signed division. That covers the only overflow case. Also, if denominator == 0, Java would throw ArithmeticException. But factorial never 0. So okay.

But do we need to go that far? Probably not, but let's be thorough.

Let's implement multiply and divide with Java semantics.

static int multiply(int a, int b) {
    return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
}

static int divide(int a, int b) {
    if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
        return a; // Java overflow behavior
    }
    return a / b; // C++ truncates toward zero, same as Java for defined cases
}

Then count:
int numerator = factorial(n);
int denom = multiply(factorial(n-m), factorial(m));
return divide(numerator, denom);

But wait, Java's expression factorial(n) / (factorial(n-m) * factorial(m)) evaluates factorial(n) first, then factorial(n-m), factorial(m), multiply, then divide. The multiplication of the two factorials is int multiplication. Our multiply does that. Good.

In C++, the equivalent of a list of strings is `std::vector<std::string>`. The Java code's `datas` field is a reference to a list. In C++, if we want to preserve the "reference to external list" semantics, we could use `std::reference_wrapper<const std::vector<std::string>>`? But reference_wrapper is copyable and assignable, but it still requires the referenced object to outlive. It doesn't own. That might be a good mapping: store `std::reference_wrapper<const std::vector<std::string>> datas;` and constructor takes `const std::vector<std::string>& datas : datas(datas) {}`. Then the class is copyable (reference_wrapper is copyable). If the original vector is modified, the object sees it. If the vector is destroyed, dangling. In Java, the object keeps it alive. But reference_wrapper doesn't. However, it's closer than copying. But the constructor with a temporary vector would dangle. In Java, a temporary list can be passed and the object keeps it alive. In C++, a temporary vector passed to a const ref constructor would be destroyed at the end of the full expression, leaving dangling reference. That's a serious issue. If we store by value, it's safe. So value semantics is safer.

Given the prompt's emphasis on behavior, I think they expect value semantics because it's the idiomatic C++ way and avoids lifetime issues. The "stateful behavior" likely refers to the object's own state (datas) being used in select, not external aliasing. So I'll use value.

Let's produce:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; ++i) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex); ++i) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(i + 1, resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
    if (b == 0) {
        throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
    }
    if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
        return a;
    }
    return a / b;
}

Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `resultIndex + 1` could overflow. As discussed, no. But to be safe, we could also wrap: `resultIndex + 1` if resultIndex = INT_MAX, but base case would have caught resultIndex == m, and m = INT_MAX, so resultIndex would be INT_MAX only at base case, not in recursion. If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

Now, let's consider if `dataIndex` could be negative due to wrap from i+1 when i = INT_MAX. In Java, if i = INT_MAX, i+1 = INT_MIN. Then recursive call with dataIndex = INT_MIN. In _select, loop starts at i = INT_MIN. The condition `i <= datas.size() - (m - resultIndex)`. Since datas.size() is non-negative and m - resultIndex >= 0, the bound is <= INT_MAX. INT_MIN <= bound true, so loop would run with i = INT_MIN, and `datas.at(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException because i is negative. In Java, `datas.get(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException. In C++, `datas.at(i)` with i negative (converted to size_t huge) would throw std::out_of_range. So behavior is similar. Good.

Now, let's consider if `selectAll` loop with n = INT_MAX and i wraps to INT_MIN, then `select(i)` with i negative would throw invalid_argument. In Java, `select(i)` with i negative would throw IllegalArgumentException. So similar. Good.

But before final, let's ensure the code compiles. We'll include necessary headers.

Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `resultIndex + 1` could overflow. As discussed, no. But to be safe, we could also wrap: `resultIndex + 1` if resultIndex = INT_MAX, but base case would have caught resultIndex == m, and m = INT_MAX, so resultIndex would be INT_MAX only at base case, not in recursion. If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

Now, let's consider if `dataIndex` could be negative due to wrap from i+1 when i = INT_MAX. In Java, if i = INT_MAX, i+1 = INT_MIN. Then recursive call with dataIndex = INT_MIN. In _select, loop starts at i = INT_MIN. The condition `i <= datas.size() - (m - resultIndex)`. Since datas.size() is non-negative and m - resultIndex >= 0, the bound is <= INT_MAX. INT_MIN <= bound true, so loop would run with i = INT_MIN, and `datas.at(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException because i is negative. In Java, `datas.get(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException. In C++, `datas.at(i)` with i negative (converted to size_t huge) would throw std::out_of_range. So behavior is similar. Good.

What if we store a `std::vector<std::string>* datas;` and constructor takes `std::vector<std::string>& datas`? Then the object doesn't own the data, but it's a reference. Copying the object copies the pointer, so both share the same data. This preserves aliasing. The constructor signature is `CombinationCalculator(std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(&datas) {}`. In Java, the parameter is `List<String> datas`, which is a reference. In C++, taking a non-const reference is similar. But Java allows passing null; C++ reference doesn't. If we want to allow null, we'd take pointer. But null is an edge case. The Java code doesn't handle null; it would NPE later. In C++, if we take a reference, null cannot be passed, so no NPE. What if we store a `std::shared_ptr<std::vector<std::string>>` and have constructor take `std::vector<std::string>& datas` by creating a non-owning shared_ptr? Actually we can create a shared_ptr that owns nothing but points to the vector: `std::shared_ptr<std::vector<std::string>> p(std::shared_ptr<std::vector<std::string>>(), &datas);` This is a non-owning alias. But then if the vector is destroyed, the shared_ptr dangles, and the object doesn't keep it alive. Java would keep it alive. So not identical.

If we want to keep the vector alive and share, we need the caller to use shared_ptr. That's a bigger API change.

Given the trade-offs, I think value semantics is the most pragmatic and safe. The prompt's "stateful behavior" likely refers to the internal state of the object (the datas list) being used consistently, not external aliasing. The class doesn't expose any way to mutate datas, so the copy is effectively immutable. The only way to see a difference is if the caller modifies the original list after construction. That's an unusual pattern. I'll stick with value.

The prompt didn't specify memory management. This is a closer mapping. This is actually a good compromise: same constructor signature, preserves aliasing, no copy. The lifetime issue is a potential dangling pointer if the vector is destroyed before the object. But in Java, the object keeps it alive, so if the vector goes out of scope, Java still works. In C++, it would dangle. That's a behavioral difference in memory safety. But if the vector is a local variable that goes out of scope, the Java object would keep it alive because the List object is referenced. In C++, if we store a pointer, the vector is destroyed when it goes out of scope, and the pointer dangles. So not identical. To keep it alive, we need ownership. So value semantics is actually more behavior-preserving in terms of lifetime: the object owns its own copy, so it works even if the original is destroyed. But it changes aliasing. Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `resultIndex + 1` could overflow. As discussed, no. But to be safe, we could also wrap: `resultIndex + 1` if resultIndex = INT_MAX, but base case would have caught resultIndex == m, and m = INT_MAX, so resultIndex would be INT_MAX only at base case, not in recursion. If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

Now, let's consider if `dataIndex` could be negative due to wrap from i+1 when i = INT_MAX. In Java, if i = INT_MAX, i+1 = INT_MIN. Then recursive call with dataIndex = INT_MIN. In _select, loop starts at i = INT_MIN. The condition `i <= datas.size() - (m - resultIndex)`. Since datas.size() is non-negative and m - resultIndex >= 0, the bound is <= INT_MAX. INT_MIN <= bound true, so loop would run with i = INT_MIN, and `datas.at(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException because i is negative. In Java, `datas.get(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException. In C++, `datas.at(i)` with i negative (converted to size_t huge) would throw std::out_of_range. So behavior is similar. Good.

Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `resultIndex + 1` could overflow. As discussed, no. But to be safe, we could also wrap: `resultIndex + 1` if resultIndex = INT_MAX, but base case would have caught resultIndex == m, and m = INT_MAX, so resultIndex would be INT_MAX only at base case, not in recursion. If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

Now, let's consider if `dataIndex` could be negative due to wrap from i+1 when i = INT_MAX. In Java, if i = INT_MAX, i+1 = INT_MIN. Then recursive call with dataIndex = INT_MIN. In _select, loop starts at i = INT_MIN. The condition `i <= datas.size() - (m - resultIndex)`. Since datas.size() is non-negative and m - resultIndex >= 0, the bound is <= INT_MAX. INT_MIN <= bound true, so loop would run with i = INT_MIN, and `datas.at(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException because i is negative. In Java, `datas.get(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException. In C++, `datas.at(i)` with i negative (converted to size_t huge) would throw std::out_of_range. So behavior is similar. Good.

But before final, let's ensure the code compiles. We'll include necessary headers.

Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

But before final, let's ensure the code compiles. We'll include necessary headers.

Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

What if we store a `std::shared_ptr<std::vector<std::string>>` and have constructor take `std::vector<std::string>& datas` by creating a non-owning shared_ptr? If we want to keep the vector alive and share, we need the caller to use shared_ptr. That's a bigger API change.

But if the vector is a local variable that goes out of scope, the Java object would keep it alive because the List object is referenced. In C++, if we store a pointer, the vector is destroyed when it goes out of scope, and the pointer dangles. So not identical. To keep it alive, we need ownership. Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `resultIndex + 1` could overflow. As discussed, no. But to be safe, we could also wrap: `resultIndex + 1` if resultIndex = INT_MAX, but base case would have caught resultIndex == m, and m = INT_MAX, so resultIndex would be INT_MAX only at base case, not in recursion. If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

Now, let's consider if `dataIndex` could be negative due to wrap from i+1 when i = INT_MAX. In Java, if i = INT_MAX, i+1 = INT_MIN. Then recursive call with dataIndex = INT_MIN. In _select, loop starts at i = INT_MIN. The condition `i <= datas.size() - (m - resultIndex)`. Since datas.size() is non-negative and m - resultIndex >= 0, the bound is <= INT_MAX. INT_MIN <= bound true, so loop would run with i = INT_MIN, and `datas.at(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException because i is negative. In Java, `datas.get(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException. In C++, `datas.at(i)` with i negative (converted to size_t huge) would throw std::out_of_range. So behavior is similar. Good.

In Java, the parameter is `List<String> datas`, which is a reference. In C++, taking a non-const reference is similar. But Java allows passing null; C++ reference doesn't. What if we store a `std::shared_ptr<std::vector<std::string>>` and have constructor take `std::vector<std::string>& datas` by creating a non-owning shared_ptr? Actually we can create a shared_ptr that owns nothing but points to the vector: `std::shared_ptr<std::vector<std::string>> p(std::shared_ptr<std::vector<std::string>>(), &datas);` This is a non-owning alias. But then if the vector is destroyed, the shared_ptr dangles, and the object doesn't keep it alive. Java would keep it alive. So not identical.

If we want to keep the vector alive and share, we need the caller to use shared_ptr. That's a bigger API change.

But in Java, the object keeps it alive, so if the vector goes out of scope, Java still works. In C++, it would dangle. That's a behavioral difference in memory safety. But if the vector is a local variable that goes out of scope, the Java object would keep it alive because the List object is referenced. In C++, if we store a pointer, the vector is destroyed when it goes out of scope, and the pointer dangles. So not identical. To keep it alive, we need ownership. So value semantics is actually more behavior-preserving in terms of lifetime: the object owns its own copy, so it works even if the original is destroyed. But it changes aliasing. Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `resultIndex + 1` could overflow. As discussed, no. But to be safe, we could also wrap: `resultIndex + 1` if resultIndex = INT_MAX, but base case would have caught resultIndex == m, and m = INT_MAX, so resultIndex would be INT_MAX only at base case, not in recursion. If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

Now, let's consider if `dataIndex` could be negative due to wrap from i+1 when i = INT_MAX. In Java, if i = INT_MAX, i+1 = INT_MIN. Then recursive call with dataIndex = INT_MIN. In _select, loop starts at i = INT_MIN. The condition `i <= datas.size() - (m - resultIndex)`. Since datas.size() is non-negative and m - resultIndex >= 0, the bound is <= INT_MAX. INT_MIN <= bound true, so loop would run with i = INT_MIN, and `datas.at(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException because i is negative. In Java, `datas.get(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException. In C++, `datas.at(i)` with i negative (converted to size_t huge) would throw std::out_of_range. So behavior is similar. Good.

But in Java, the object keeps it alive, so if the vector goes out of scope, Java still works. In C++, it would dangle. That's a behavioral difference in memory safety. But if the vector is a local variable that goes out of scope, the Java object would keep it alive because the List object is referenced. In C++, if we store a pointer, the vector is destroyed when it goes out of scope, and the pointer dangles. So not identical. To keep it alive, we need ownership. So value semantics is actually more behavior-preserving in terms of lifetime: the object owns its own copy, so it works even if the original is destroyed. But it changes aliasing. Which is more important? The prompt says "side effects" and "stateful behavior". If the original vector is modified after construction, Java sees changes; C++ value doesn't. If the original vector is destroyed, Java still works; C++ value works, pointer doesn't. Both are differences. There's no perfect mapping without shared_ptr. Shared_ptr preserves both aliasing and lifetime, but changes constructor signature. Let's consider using shared_ptr and providing a constructor that takes `std::vector<std::string>` by value? No.

Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `resultIndex + 1` could overflow. As discussed, no. But to be safe, we could also wrap: `resultIndex + 1` if resultIndex = INT_MAX, but base case would have caught resultIndex == m, and m = INT_MAX, so resultIndex would be INT_MAX only at base case, not in recursion. If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

Now, let's consider if `dataIndex` could be negative due to wrap from i+1 when i = INT_MAX. In Java, if i = INT_MAX, i+1 = INT_MIN. Then recursive call with dataIndex = INT_MIN. In _select, loop starts at i = INT_MIN. The condition `i <= datas.size() - (m - resultIndex)`. Since datas.size() is non-negative and m - resultIndex >= 0, the bound is <= INT_MAX. INT_MIN <= bound true, so loop would run with i = INT_MIN, and `datas.at(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException because i is negative. In Java, `datas.get(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException. In C++, `datas.at(i)` with i negative (converted to size_t huge) would throw std::out_of_range. So behavior is similar. Good.
