Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `resultIndex + 1` could overflow. As discussed, no. But to be safe, we could also wrap: `resultIndex + 1` if resultIndex = INT_MAX, but base case would have caught resultIndex == m, and m = INT_MAX, so resultIndex would be INT_MAX only at base case, not in recursion. If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

Now, let's consider if `dataIndex` could be negative due to wrap from i+1 when i = INT_MAX. In Java, if i = INT_MAX, i+1 = INT_MIN. Then recursive call with dataIndex = INT_MIN. In _select, loop starts at i = INT_MIN. The condition `i <= datas.size() - (m - resultIndex)`. Since datas.size() is non-negative and m - resultIndex >= 0, the bound is <= INT_MAX. INT_MIN <= bound true, so loop would run with i = INT_MIN, and `datas.at(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException because i is negative. In Java, `datas.get(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException. In C++, `datas.at(i)` with i negative (converted to size_t huge) would throw std::out_of_range. So behavior is similar. Good.

Now, let's consider if `selectAll` loop with n = INT_MAX and i wraps to INT_MIN, then `select(i)` with i negative would throw invalid_argument. In Java, `select(i)` with i negative would throw IllegalArgumentException. So similar. Good.

But before final, let's ensure the code compiles. We'll include necessary headers.

Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `resultIndex + 1` could overflow. As discussed, no. But to be safe, we could also wrap: `resultIndex + 1` if resultIndex = INT_MAX, but base case would have caught resultIndex == m, and m = INT_MAX, so resultIndex would be INT_MAX only at base case, not in recursion. If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

But if the vector is a local variable that goes out of scope, the Java object would keep it alive because the List object is referenced. In C++, if we store a pointer, the vector is destroyed when it goes out of scope, and the pointer dangles. So not identical. To keep it alive, we need ownership. Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `resultIndex + 1` could overflow. As discussed, no. But to be safe, we could also wrap: `resultIndex + 1` if resultIndex = INT_MAX, but base case would have caught resultIndex == m, and m = INT_MAX, so resultIndex would be INT_MAX only at base case, not in recursion. If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

INT_MIN <= bound true, so loop would run with i = INT_MIN, and `datas.at(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException because i is negative. In Java, `datas.get(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException. In C++, `datas.at(i)` with i negative (converted to size_t huge) would throw std::out_of_range. So behavior is similar. Good.

What if we store a `std::vector<std::string>* datas;` and constructor takes `std::vector<std::string>& datas`? Then the object doesn't own the data, but it's a reference. Copying the object copies the pointer, so both share the same data. This preserves aliasing. The constructor signature is `CombinationCalculator(std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(&datas) {}`. In Java, the parameter is `List<String> datas`, which is a reference. In C++, taking a non-const reference is similar. But Java allows passing null; C++ reference doesn't. If we want to allow null, we'd take pointer. But null is an edge case. The Java code doesn't handle null; it would NPE later. In C++, if we take a reference, null cannot be passed, so no NPE. What if we store a `std::shared_ptr<std::vector<std::string>>` and have constructor take `std::vector<std::string>& datas` by creating a non-owning shared_ptr? Actually we can create a shared_ptr that owns nothing but points to the vector: `std::shared_ptr<std::vector<std::string>> p(std::shared_ptr<std::vector<std::string>>(), &datas);` This is a non-owning alias. But then if the vector is destroyed, the shared_ptr dangles, and the object doesn't keep it alive. Java would keep it alive. So not identical.

If we want to keep the vector alive and share, we need the caller to use shared_ptr. That's a bigger API change.

Given the trade-offs, I think value semantics is the most pragmatic and safe. The prompt's "stateful behavior" likely refers to the internal state of the object (the datas list) being used consistently, not external aliasing. The class doesn't expose any way to mutate datas, so the copy is effectively immutable. The only way to see a difference is if the caller modifies the original list after construction. That's an unusual pattern. I'll stick with value.

The object doesn't own the data, but if the original vector outlives the object, behavior is identical. The Java object would keep the vector alive; in C++, the user must ensure. The prompt didn't specify memory management. This is a closer mapping. The constructor signature is `CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas)` which is the same as the value version. The only difference is the member is a pointer. This is actually a good compromise: same constructor signature, preserves aliasing, no copy. The lifetime issue is a potential dangling pointer if the vector is destroyed before the object. But in Java, the object keeps it alive, so if the vector goes out of scope, Java still works. In C++, it would dangle. That's a behavioral difference in memory safety. But if the vector is a local variable that goes out of scope, the Java object would keep it alive because the List object is referenced. In C++, if we store a pointer, the vector is destroyed when it goes out of scope, and the pointer dangles. So not identical. To keep it alive, we need ownership. So value semantics is actually more behavior-preserving in terms of lifetime: the object owns its own copy, so it works even if the original is destroyed. But it changes aliasing. Which is more important? The prompt says "side effects" and "stateful behavior". If the original vector is modified after construction, Java sees changes; C++ value doesn't. Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `resultIndex + 1` could overflow. As discussed, no. But to be safe, we could also wrap: `resultIndex + 1` if resultIndex = INT_MAX, but base case would have caught resultIndex == m, and m = INT_MAX, so resultIndex would be INT_MAX only at base case, not in recursion. If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

Now, let's consider if `dataIndex` could be negative due to wrap from i+1 when i = INT_MAX. In Java, if i = INT_MAX, i+1 = INT_MIN. Then recursive call with dataIndex = INT_MIN. In _select, loop starts at i = INT_MIN. The condition `i <= datas.size() - (m - resultIndex)`. Since datas.size() is non-negative and m - resultIndex >= 0, the bound is <= INT_MAX. INT_MIN <= bound true, so loop would run with i = INT_MIN, and `datas.at(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException because i is negative. In Java, `datas.get(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException. In C++, `datas.at(i)` with i negative (converted to size_t huge) would throw std::out_of_range. So behavior is similar. Good.

The prompt didn't specify memory management. This is a closer mapping. The constructor signature is `CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas)` which is the same as the value version. The only difference is the member is a pointer. This is actually a good compromise: same constructor signature, preserves aliasing, no copy. The lifetime issue is a potential dangling pointer if the vector is destroyed before the object. But in Java, the object keeps it alive, so if the vector goes out of scope, Java still works. In C++, it would dangle. That's a behavioral difference in memory safety. But if the vector is a local variable that goes out of scope, the Java object would keep it alive because the List object is referenced. In C++, if we store a pointer, the vector is destroyed when it goes out of scope, and the pointer dangles. So not identical. To keep it alive, we need ownership. So value semantics is actually more behavior-preserving in terms of lifetime: the object owns its own copy, so it works even if the original is destroyed. But it changes aliasing. Which is more important? The prompt says "side effects" and "stateful behavior". If the original vector is modified after construction, Java sees changes; C++ value doesn't. If the original vector is destroyed, Java still works; C++ value works, pointer doesn't. Both are differences. There's no perfect mapping without shared_ptr. Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Code:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

Wait, in `_select`, the recursive call uses `i + 1` with wrap. But the loop increment also uses `i + 1` with wrap. In Java, the recursive call is `_select(i + 1, ...)` and the loop increment is `i++`. Both use the same next value. So using `static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)` for both is correct. However, note that in the loop, after the recursive call returns, the loop increment will compute the same next value again. In Java, the recursive call receives `i + 1` (the next value), and then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` increments i to the same next value. So yes, both should be the same. Good.

But there's a subtlety: In Java, the recursive call `_select(i + 1, ...)` is evaluated with the current i. If i = INT_MAX, `i + 1` overflows to INT_MIN. Our wrap expression does that. Then after the recursive call, the loop's `i++` also wraps to INT_MIN. So the next iteration uses INT_MIN. In Java, after `i++`, i becomes INT_MIN. So same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `resultIndex + 1` could overflow. As discussed, no. But to be safe, we could also wrap: `resultIndex + 1` if resultIndex = INT_MAX, but base case would have caught resultIndex == m, and m = INT_MAX, so resultIndex would be INT_MAX only at base case, not in recursion. If m = INT_MAX and resultIndex = INT_MAX - 1, +1 = INT_MAX, no overflow. So fine.

Now, let's consider if `dataIndex` could be negative due to wrap from i+1 when i = INT_MAX. In Java, if i = INT_MAX, i+1 = INT_MIN. Then recursive call with dataIndex = INT_MIN. In _select, loop starts at i = INT_MIN. The condition `i <= datas.size() - (m - resultIndex)`. Since datas.size() is non-negative and m - resultIndex >= 0, the bound is <= INT_MAX. INT_MIN <= bound true, so loop would run with i = INT_MIN, and `datas.at(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException because i is negative. In Java, `datas.get(i)` would throw IndexOutOfBoundsException. In C++, `datas.at(i)` with i negative (converted to size_t huge) would throw std::out_of_range. So behavior is similar. Good.

Now, let's consider if `selectAll` loop with n = INT_MAX and i wraps to INT_MIN, then `select(i)` with i negative would throw invalid_argument. In Java, `select(i)` with i negative would throw IllegalArgumentException. So similar. Good.

Now, let's think about the `count` function's `factorial(n)` where n is int. If n = INT_MAX, factorial loops infinitely. That's Java behavior. Good.

Now, let's consider if `count` with n = INT_MAX, m = 0 returns 1 before factorial. Good.

Now, let's consider if `count` with n = INT_MAX, m = INT_MAX returns 1 before factorial. Good.

Now, let's consider if `count` with n = INT_MAX, m = 1: factorial(INT_MAX) infinite loop. Java same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `countAll` with n = 63 returns inf. Good.

Now, let's consider if `countAll` with n = 62 returns 1073741823.0. Good.

Now, let's consider if `countAll` with n = 32 returns 0.0. Good.

Now, let's consider if `countAll` with n = 31 returns 2147483647.0. Good.

Now, let's consider if `countAll` with n = 30 returns (1<<30)-1 = 1073741823? Wait 1<<30 = 1073741824, -1 = 1073741823. Yes. n=30 shift=30, same as n=62? Actually n=62 shift=30, so same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `countAll` with n = 63 returns infinity, but in Java, `n != 63 ? ... : Double.POSITIVE_INFINITY` means for n=63 it returns infinity. Our code returns infinity. Good.

Now, let's consider if `countAll` with n = -1 returns NaN. Good.

Now, let's consider if `countAll` with n = 64 returns NaN. Good.

Now, let's think about the `multiply` function with `uint32_t` multiplication. If `a` or `b` is negative, converting to uint32_t gives two's complement. Multiplication modulo 2^32. Cast back to int32_t gives the correct Java result. Good.

Now, let's consider if `divide` with `a` negative and `b` positive: C++ signed division truncates toward zero, same as Java. For example -3 / 2 = -1 in both. Good.

Now, let's consider if `divide` with `a` positive and `b` negative: same.

Now, let's consider if `divide` with `a` negative and `b` negative: same.

Now, let's consider if `divide` with `a` INT_MIN and `b` -1: we return a (INT_MIN). Java returns INT_MIN. Good.

Now, let's consider if `divide` with `a` INT_MIN and `b` 1: C++ returns INT_MIN, Java returns INT_MIN. Good.

Now, let's consider if `divide` with `a` INT_MIN and `b` 0: we throw before. Good.

Now, let's consider if `factorial` with x negative returns 1. Our loop `for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = ...)` with x negative: condition 1 <= x false, returns 1. Good.

Now, let's consider if `count` with n negative, m positive: factorial(n)=1, factorial(n-m) with n-m negative =1, factorial(m) maybe. So result = 1 / m!. If m! > 1, integer division truncates to 0. Java same. Good.

Now, let's consider if `count` with n negative, m negative: if n == m, returns 1. Else factorial(n)=1, factorial(n-m) with n-m maybe negative or positive. If n=-1, m=-2: n!=m, factorial(-1)=1, factorial(1)=1, factorial(-2)=1, result 1. Java same. Good.

Now, let's think about the `select` method's `resultList.reserve(m)`. If m is 0, reserve(0) is fine. If m is negative, we throw. Good.

Now, let's consider if `select` with m > 0 and datas.size() = 0: resultList.reserve(m) may allocate memory. In Java, new ArrayList<>(m) allocates an array of size m, which could be large. In C++, reserve(m) allocates capacity for m elements. Both may throw if m is huge. Good.

Now, let's think about the `selectAll` method: In Java, `for (int i = 1; i <= datas.size(); i++)`. If datas.size() is 0, loop not executed. If datas.size() is Integer.MAX_VALUE, i++ from MAX_VALUE to MIN_VALUE? Actually if i = Integer.MAX_VALUE, after loop body, i++ overflows to MIN_VALUE, condition i <= size (MIN_VALUE <= MAX_VALUE) true, infinite loop. In C++, if we use `for (int i = 1; i <= n; ++i)` with n = INT_MAX, ++i overflow UB. To mimic Java, we should use the same wrap increment. But again, this is an extreme edge case. To be safe, we can write:

The `count` doesn't have loops except factorial. So let's update `_select` loop:

#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};

=== TRANSLATED CODE (cpp) ===
#include <vector>
#include <string>
#include <cstdint>
#include <limits>
#include <stdexcept>

class CombinationCalculator {
private:
    std::vector<std::string> datas;

    static int32_t multiply(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        return static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(a) * static_cast<uint32_t>(b));
    }

    static int32_t divide(int32_t a, int32_t b) {
        if (b == 0) {
            throw std::runtime_error("/ by zero");
        }
        if (b == -1 && a == std::numeric_limits<int32_t>::min()) {
            return a;
        }
        return a / b;
    }

    static int32_t factorial(int32_t x) {
        int32_t result = 1;
        for (int32_t i = 1; i <= x; i = static_cast<int32_t>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            result = multiply(result, i);
        }
        return result;
    }

public:
    CombinationCalculator(const std::vector<std::string>& datas) : datas(datas) {}

    static int count(int n, int m) {
        if (m == 0 || n == m) {
            return 1;
        }
        return divide(factorial(n), multiply(factorial(n - m), factorial(m)));
    }

    static double countAll(int n) {
        if (n < 0 || n > 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::quiet_NaN();
        }
        if (n == 63) {
            return std::numeric_limits<double>::infinity();
        }
        uint32_t u = (static_cast<uint32_t>(1) << (n & 31)) - 1;
        return static_cast<double>(static_cast<int32_t>(u));
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> select(int m) {
        if (m < 0) {
            throw std::invalid_argument("Illegal Capacity: " + std::to_string(m));
        }
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        std::vector<std::string> resultList;
        resultList.reserve(m);
        _select(0, resultList, 0, result, m);
        return result;
    }

    std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selectAll() {
        std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> result;
        int n = static_cast<int>(datas.size());
        for (int i = 1; i <= n; i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            std::vector<std::vector<std::string>> selected = select(i);
            result.insert(result.end(), selected.begin(), selected.end());
        }
        return result;
    }

protected:
    void _select(int dataIndex, std::vector<std::string>& resultList, int resultIndex,
                 std::vector<std::vector<std::string>>& result, int m) {
        if (resultIndex == m) {
            result.push_back(resultList);
            return;
        }
        for (int i = dataIndex; i <= static_cast<int>(datas.size()) - (m - resultIndex);
             i = static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1)) {
            resultList.insert(resultList.begin() + resultIndex, datas.at(i));
            _select(static_cast<int>(static_cast<uint32_t>(i) + 1), resultList, resultIndex + 1, result, m);
            resultList.erase(resultList.begin() + resultIndex);
        }
    }
};
